[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. Do not quote or place tool-call markup inside explanatory text; when you decide to call the tool, emit the tool call itself.
- Snippets are not saved, so re-import or redefine needed helpers. Use as many short, purposeful calls as needed to establish a clear probe or plan.
- `action(...)` refreshes runtime state immediately. Inspection-only calls preserve both `last_action_result` and `last_animation`.
- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.

Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.

- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Interesting! After the third DOWN:
# - Orange is at [[35, 34], [35, 38], [36, 38], [36, 34]]
# - Blue is now at [[31, 21], [32, 22], [32, 21]] - which is the WHITE object's position!

print("Current nodes (small ones):")
for n in curr_seg['nodes']:
    if n['pixels'] < 50:
        color_map = {'W':'white','w':'light gray','g':'gray','G':'dark gray','c':'charcoal',

                     'Y':'yellow','O':'orange','r':'dark red','N':'light green','p':'purple'}

    action(['LEFT'])
    curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
    orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None

        print("LEVEL COMPLETED!")
        break

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. - `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.

Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.

Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. - Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.

Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.

If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.

- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

# After the sequence:
# - UP didn't move (orange stuck at row 15-16)
# - DOWN moved orange to row 20-21
# - LEFT didn't move (stuck at col 34-38)
# - RIGHT didn't move (stuck at col 34-38)

# The orange can only move vertically, not horizontally from its current position.
# This suggests there's a vertical channel or corridor it's in.

# Looking at the image, I see the orange/blue objects are in a vertical strip
# between the gray area on the left and the right edge of the screen.

# Let me try moving DOWN further to see if the orange can reach the bottom
# or trigger something.

for i in range(10):
    action(['DOWN'])
    curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
    orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
    blue_obj = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['id'] == 7][0] if any(n['id'] == 7 for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
    print(f"DOWN #{i+1}: Orange={orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}, Blue={blue_obj['boundary'] if blue_obj else 'None'}")
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed'):
        print("LEVEL COMPLETED!")
        break
    if last_action_result.get('game_over'):
        print("GAME OVER!")
        break

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# LEFT didn't move the orange - it's still at col 34-38. The blue is at [[32, 20]].

# I've been trying many approaches without success. Let me step back and think
# about what the actual goal might be.

# Looking at the pattern of resets:
# 1. When blue reaches white object position -> scene resets
# 2. Objects move in 5-row/column increments
# 3. The yellow timer bar is shrinking

# Maybe the goal is NOT to complete a traditional puzzle, but rather to survive
# until the timer runs out? Or maybe there's a specific number of moves needed?

# Alternatively, maybe I need to look at this from a completely different angle.
# What if the "player" is not controlling the orange/blue objects, but rather
# something else entirely?

# Let me check if there's any change in the top black box area when I move objects.
# Maybe the goal is to fill the black box with orange/blue pixels?

curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
# Check the black box (ID 3) and its children
black_box = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['id'] == 3][0] if any(n['id'] == 3 for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
print(f"Black box (ID 3): {black_box['boundary'] if black_box else 'None'}")

# Check children of black box
if black_box:
    # Find nodes that are inside the black box boundary
    bb_boundary = black_box['boundary']
    print(f"Black box boundary: {bb_boundary}")

# Let me try a different approach: what if I need to move the objects to a
# specific final position? Let me try moving them to the very top (row 9-14)
# where the black box is.

print("\nTrying to move orange into black box area...")
for i in range(3):
    action(['UP'])
    curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
    orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
    print(f"UP #{i+1}: Orange={orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}")
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed'):
        print("LEVEL COMPLETED!")
        break

# The orange stopped at row 45-46 again. I've been going in circles.

# Let me think about this differently. I've tried:
# - Moving objects toward the white object -> causes reset
# - Moving objects to the top black box -> can't enter
# - Moving objects to the bottom -> stops at row 45-46
# - Moving horizontally -> doesn't work from most positions

# Maybe the goal is something entirely different. Let me look at the image
# one more time and consider what might be missing.

# Looking at the current state:
# - Orange is at the bottom of its vertical channel (row 45-46)
# - Blue is somewhere (need to check)
# - White object is in the middle
# - Yellow timer bar is very small

# What if the goal is related to the TIMER? Maybe I need to wait for it to
# run out, or maybe I need to complete something before it runs out?

# Or perhaps there's a hidden mechanic I haven't discovered yet.

# Let me try one more thing: what if I need to move the objects in a specific
# pattern or sequence that I haven't tried yet?

# Let me check the current state first
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
blue_obj = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['id'] == 11][0] if any(n['id'] == 11 for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
white_obj = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['id'] == 7][0] if any(n['id'] == 7 for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
print(f"Orange: {orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}")
print(f"Blue: {blue_obj['boundary'] if blue_obj else 'None'}")
print(f"White: {white_obj['boundary'] if white_obj else 'None'}")

# Let me try moving LEFT from the bottom position to see if horizontal movement
# is possible there
print("\nTrying LEFT from bottom position...")
action(['LEFT'])
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
print(f"After LEFT: Orange={orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}")

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.

# EXCELLENT! LEFT worked from the bottom position!
# - Orange moved from col 34-38 to col 29-33 (moved LEFT by 5 columns)

# This is a breakthrough! The orange can move horizontally when it's at the
# bottom of its vertical channel. This suggests there's a horizontal corridor
# at the bottom that I haven't explored before.

# Now let me continue moving LEFT to see where the orange can go.
print("Continuing LEFT...")
for i in range(5):
    action(['LEFT'])
    curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
    orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
    print(f"LEFT #{i+1}: Orange={orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}")
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed'):
        print("LEVEL COMPLETED!")
        break

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. - Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.

for n in curr_seg['nodes']:
    if n['pixels'] < 50:
        color_map = {'W':'white','w':'light gray','g':'gray','G':'dark gray','c':'charcoal',
                     'B':'black','M':'magenta','P':'pink','R':'red','b':'blue','S':'sky blue',
                     'Y':'yellow','O':'orange','r':'dark red','N':'light green','p':'purple'}

# the current position does anything.

    curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

<parameter=code>
# After moving UP:
# - The orange moved from col 24-28 back to col 34-38 (the right-side channel)
# - It's now at row 20-21, which is in the middle of the vertical channel

# I notice something interesting: when I pressed UP from the bottom-left position,
# the orange first stayed stuck (UP #1-4), then suddenly jumped to the right-side
# channel (UP #5). This suggests there might be a teleport or warp mechanism.

# Looking at the current image, the scene looks very similar to earlier states.
# The yellow timer bar is almost empty now.

# Let me check if there's been any progress. Maybe I need to look at this from
# a completely different angle.

# Actually, let me reconsider the puzzle. I've been assuming the goal is to move
# the orange/blue objects around, but maybe the goal is something else entirely.

# Looking at the image again:
# - There's a black box with blue pixels at the top
# - Orange and blue objects that can be moved
# - A white object in the middle
# - A blue L-shape at bottom-left

# What if the goal is to get the ORANGE object to enter the TOP BLACK BOX?
# Currently, the orange is at row 20-21, and the black box is at rows 9-14.
# They're separated by several rows.

# But earlier attempts showed that the orange can't move past row 15-16 when
# trying to go UP into the black box area.

# Let me try one more thing: maybe I need to approach the black box from a
# different direction. Let me try moving LEFT first, then UP.

print("Trying LEFT then UP sequence...")
action(['LEFT'])
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
print(f"After LEFT: Orange={orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}")

# Check if LEFT worked
if orange and orange['boundary'][0][1] < 34:
    print("LEFT worked! Now trying UP...")
    for i in range(5):
        action(['UP'])
        curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation
        orange = [n for n in curr_seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O'][0] if any(n['color'] == 'O' for n in curr_seg['nodes']) else None
        print(f"UP #{i+1}: Orange={orange['boundary'] if orange else 'None'}")
        if last_action_result.get('level_completed'):
            print("LEVEL COMPLETED!")
            break
